<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which pairing correctly identifies the enzyme&#x27;s active-site proton acceptor and nucleophile?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Aspartate is the proton acceptor, and cysteine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Histidine is the proton acceptor, and cysteine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Cysteine is the proton acceptor, and glutamate is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Glutamate is the proton acceptor, and cysteine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>Analyzing the protein sequence, I need to identify the catalytic center of this enzyme.<br><br>**Step 1: Identify key motifs**<br>Scanning the sequence, I find the motif &quot;GGTLVCGGKIM&quot; which contains a catalytic cysteine in a conserved context. This &quot;CGGK&quot; pattern is characteristic of cysteine-based hydrolases/proteases.<br><br>**Step 2: Identify the nucleophile**<br>The cysteine (C) in the &quot;CGGK&quot; motif is positioned in a structurally conserved region, making it the likely nucleophile that attacks the substrate&#x27;s electrophilic center.<br><br>**Step 3: Identify the proton acceptor**<br>Looking at the broader region &quot;GLPHNASIVHTETFVP,&quot; there is a histidine (H) in the &quot;SIVHT&quot; sequence. In 3D protein structure, this histidine would be spatially proximate to the catalytic cysteine, forming a classic Cys-His catalytic dyad. The histidine acts as a general base (proton acceptor), deprotonating the cysteine&#x27;s thiol group to enhance its nucleophilicity.<br><br>**Step 4: Evaluate options**<br>- Option A (Asp + Cys): While some phosphatases use Asp-Cys pairs, the His found near the catalytic Cys makes this less likely here.<br>- Option B (His + Cys): Matches the classic cysteine protease/hydrolase catalytic dyad mechanism perfectly.<br>- Option C (Cys as proton acceptor + Glu as nucleophile): Biologically implausible — Cys rarely acts as a proton acceptor in catalysis.<br>- Option D (Glu + Cys): Glutamate is not typically found as the proton acceptor in Cys-based catalytic dyads in this context.<br><br>The His-Cys dyad is the most well-characterized and common catalytic pair for cysteine-based enzymes, and the sequence evidence supports this pairing.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this protein, assess the proposed active-site roles using side-chain chemistry, while keeping in mind that chemical plausibility alone does not establish a residue’s specific function. Which pairing is supported?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Aspartate is the proton acceptor, and cysteine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Histidine is the proton acceptor, and cysteine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Glutamate is the proton acceptor, and cysteine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Glutamate is the proton acceptor, and serine is the nucleophile.<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> active_site_0000:2<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: To determine which pairing is supported, I need to analyze both the protein sequence and the chemical plausibility of each option.<br><br>**Protein Analysis:** The sequence contains the characteristic &quot;GDPWDP&quot; motif, which is found in glycoside hydrolase families (such as GH26 β-mannanases). The protein also contains multiple Cys and His residues scattered throughout the sequence, including &quot;GSDC&quot; and &quot;GGEKHTGG&quot; regions.<br><br>**Evaluating the options:**<br><br>- **Option A (Asp proton acceptor + Cys nucleophile):** While Asp can act as a proton acceptor and Cys can be a nucleophile, this pairing is not a well-established catalytic dyad in enzymology. The pKa of Asp (~3.9) makes it a poor proton acceptor in many physiological contexts when paired with Cys.<br><br>- **Option B (His proton acceptor + Cys nucleophile):** This is the classic **His-Cys catalytic dyad**, extensively characterized in cysteine proteases (papain, caspases, calpains, etc.). The imidazole ring of His has a pKa near physiological pH (~6.0), making it an excellent proton acceptor. The thiol of Cys is a strong nucleophile, especially when deprotonated by His. This is the most well-supported pairing in enzymology literature.<br><br>- **Option C (Glu proton acceptor + Cys nucleophile):** Glu (pKa ~4.1) is typically a poor proton acceptor at physiological pH since it is predominantly deprotonated. This pairing lacks strong chemical support.<br><br>- **Option D (Glu proton acceptor + Ser nucleophile):** Ser&#x27;s hydroxyl is a weak nucleophile (requires activation), and Glu is again a poor proton acceptor. This pairing is not well-established.<br><br>**Key insight:** The question&#x27;s caveat (&quot;chemical plausibility alone does not establish a residue&#x27;s specific function&quot;) reminds us that while we should evaluate chemistry, the His-Cys dyad is the pairing most robustly supported by decades of structural and biochemical evidence. Among all options, it represents the most chemically well-characterized and mechanistically coherent catalytic pair, with His having optimal pKa for proton transfer to/from Cys&#x27;s thiol group.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>

D. &quot;The channel primarily mediates assembly of the enzyme&#x27;s subunits.&quot;
- This is incorrect. Subunit assembly is typically mediated by protein-protein interaction surfaces, not by the substrate access channel. While MAOs can form dimers, this is not the primary function of the substrate channel.

The question specifically asks to &quot;use substrate-specific evidence to distinguish an access limitation from effects on cofactor preference, flavin binding, or subunit assembly.&quot; This directly points to option A, which describes the substrate access channel&#x27;s role in substrate specificity and activity for bulky substrates.

The answer is A.
</pre>
